  T9, step 2 of 6: create the backend on Render

  2a. The Postgres version (1 minute, in Neon)
  1. In your Neon project, click SQL Editor in the left menu.
  2. Type SHOW server_version; and click Run.
  3. Note the number shown, for example 16.9 or 17.5.

  2b. Make a JWT secret, in your own Terminal app (not here with !, so it stays
  out of this chat):
  python3 -c "import secrets; print(secrets.token_urlsafe(48))"
  Save the output in your password manager as "Prod JWT_SECRET". It must be new,
  not the one in your local .env.

  2c. Create the Render service
  1. Go to render.com, click Get Started, and sign up with GitHub.
     - When GitHub asks which repositories Render may see, choose Only select 
       repositories, then drone-tender-desk.
  2. In the Render dashboard, click New +, then Web Service, then select
     drone-tender-desk.
  3. Fill in the form:

  | Field              | Enter
     |
  |--------------------|--------------------------------------------------------
  ----|
  | Name               | drone-tender-desk-api
     |
  | Region             | Ohio (US East)
     |
  | Branch             | main
     |
  | Root Directory     | backend
     |
  | Language / Runtime | Python 3
     |
  | Build Command      | uv sync --frozen --no-dev (replace whatever is
  pre-filled) |
  | Start Command      | bash render-start.sh
     |
  | Instance Type      | Free
     |

  4. Scroll to Environment Variables and add five, using Add Environment 
     Variable for each:

  | Name               | Value                                               |
  |--------------------|-----------------------------------------------------|
  | DATABASE_URL       | paste the Neon string from your password manager    |
  | JWT_SECRET         | paste the new secret from 2b                        |
  | ALLOWED_ORIGINS    | https://placeholder.invalid (we fix this in step 4) |
  | ENABLE_API_DOCS    | false                                               |
  | TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS | 1                                                   |
     Don't add PYTHON_VERSION; the repository's .python-version file sets it.
  5. Open Advanced and set Health Check Path to /api/health.
  6. Click Create Web Service (or Deploy Web Service). The log page opens. The
     first build takes a few minutes.

  What a good log looks like (near the end):
  Applying database migrations...
  ... Running upgrade  -> 0001, initial
  ... Running upgrade 0001 -> 0002, pdfs in database
  Starting the API on port ...
  ==> Your service is live

  7. At the top of the page, Render shows your address, like
     https://drone-tender-desk-api.onrender.com (the name may have extra
     letters). Open that address with /api/health added on the end.

  When you're done, reply with:
  - the Postgres version from 2a;
  - your Render address. It's public, so it's fine to share;
  - what /api/health shows. We expect {"status":"ok","database":"ok"};
  - if the build or start fails: copy the last 20 or so log lines here. First
    check they contain no password, though our code never prints one.

⏺ Before the plan: the go-live rate-limit check (check 5) is still open, and the
  live record sits on the unmerged golive-record branch. Neither blocks this

  frontend-spec.md. We can come back to them whenever you like.

  Here's the plan. No files are written yet.

  Two things to know first

  1. Pushing the theme branch creates a Vercel preview. Vercel builds every
     pushed branch at a long public "preview" address. That preview won't load 
     tenders, because the backend only accepts calls from the production
     address. That's expected and harmless. Your review happens locally, and
     nothing reaches the live site until the PR is merged into main. Merging
     triggers the production deploy, which is why I won't merge without your
     approval.
  2. "Appearance only" holds in the code too:
     - no changes to API calls, routes, data or the backend;
     - the visible text the tests rely on stays the same, such as the "Drone and
       counter-drone tenders" heading and the badge words;
     - new elements are decorative (hidden from screen readers) or are plain new
       text (the tagline and the footer).

  Contrast rule: body text at least 4.5:1 against its background, and large text
  and button edges at least 3:1 (the WCAG AA standard). A test computes these
  ratios from the actual tokens, so a later colour tweak can't silently break
  readability.

  How to view it locally

  Your local database, backend and frontend are already running, serving the
  theme branch with your local sample tenders.

  1. Desktop: open http://localhost:5173. Look at:
     - the home page with the banner;
     - Status: All, to see the green Open, amber Closing soon and grey Closed
       badges;
     - a tender's detail page, for the radar-shield icon by Category;
     - http://localhost:5173/admin/login.
  2. Phone size: in Chrome, press Cmd+Option+I, click the device toolbar icon (a
     phone and tablet), and pick iPhone SE (375 px). Reload and repeat step 1.
  3. Reduced motion: the radar sweep turns slowly. To see it still: in DevTools
     press Cmd+Shift+P, type "reduced motion", choose Emulate CSS 
     prefers-reduced-motion: reduce, and reload.
  4. The admin pages: your local database has no admin yet. Create one in your
     own Terminal app, then log in at /admin/login:
  cd ~/projects/drone-tender-desk/backend && uv run python -m app.cli 
  create-admin
  5. Screenshots (home, detail, login and not-found, at desktop and phone sizes)
     are in one folder. Open it with:
  ! open /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-dakshaya/7354c12c-6317-4fd4-a71a-a31356e
  90d2e/scratchpad/theme-review

  If the servers have stopped (for example after a restart), start them with
  docker compose up -d db --wait. Then run cd backend && uv run uvicorn 
  app.main:app --port 8000 in one terminal, and cd frontend && npm run dev in
  another.

※ recap: The site is live with the new theme, and we're on go-live check 5, which tests that the login rate limit sees each visitor's real address. Next: run steps a to c within one minute and send me the results.

!  for i in 1 2 3 4 5 6; do curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code} ' -X POST     
  https://drone-tender-desk.onrender.com/api/admin/login -H 'Content-Type:

  ⎿  401 401 401 401 401 401

⏺ That's six 401s with no 429, so the 6th attempt wasn't blocked. You can skip 
  steps b and c for now. This first result already shows a problem, and it's a
  real one: on the live site, login attempts aren't being rate-limited at all.

  What it means: the backend counted each of your 6 attempts as coming from a
  different address. With TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS = 1, the app reads the last address
  in the forwarding chain. Two hosts sit in front of the app: Cloudflare (we
  saw server: cloudflare), then Render's own proxy. The chain probably looks
  like this:

  X-Forwarded-For: <your laptop>, <Cloudflare server>
                        ↑ 2nd from the right    ↑ last (what we read now)

  The last entry is a Cloudflare server, and that changes from request to
  request, so every attempt looks like a new visitor. The real address is most
  likely second from the right, so the setting should probably be 2.

  That reasoning is my best guess from the evidence, not something I can see
  directly. So we'll measure it: set it to 2 and rerun the same three steps.
  - If 2 is right, step a will block the 6th attempt and b will let your phone
    in.
  - Step c then guards against going too far. If the setting reached past the
    real address into the faked one, c would get through, and we'd know it was
    wrong.

  Change one setting in Render

  1. Go to dashboard.render.com and click your service, drone-tender-desk.
  2. In the left menu, click Environment.
  3. Find TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS, click its edit (pencil) icon, and change the value
     from 1 to 2.
  4. Click Save, rebuild and deploy (or Save changes, then confirm the deploy if
     asked).
  5. Click Events or Logs and wait until it says "Your service is live", usually
     1–3 minutes.

  Reply "deployed" when it's live. I'll then confirm the backend is up, and
  you'll rerun steps a, b and c together.

⏺ Still six 401s, so changing the setting to 2 didn't fix it. My guess about the
  forwarding chain was wrong, and the live login is still not rate-limited.
  Skip step b; it can't tell us anything until step a works.

  I'd rather not keep guessing values. Each guess costs a redeploy, and a wrong
  guess in the other direction would let attackers fake their address. The
  problem is that I can't see the forwarding information the app actually 
  receives. All I know is that whatever we read changes from request to request.
  Any of these would explain that:
  - there are more proxies in the chain than 2;
  - the chain is shorter than the setting, so the app falls back to Render's own
    changing proxy address;
  - Cloudflare passes your address in a different header, such as
    CF-Connecting-IP, rather than in X-Forwarded-For.

  Proposal: measure it with a temporary diagnostic log line

  1. Code (small, safe, off by default):
  - A new setting, LOG_CLIENT_IP_DIAGNOSTICS, defaulting to false.
  - When it's on, each login attempt writes one line to Render's log showing:
    - how many entries X-Forwarded-For has;
    - which forwarding headers are present (CF-Connecting-IP, True-Client-IP,
      X-Real-IP);
    - a short anonymised fingerprint (6 characters of a salted hash) for each
      address, never the address itself.
  - No passwords and no usernames are logged.
  - It ships with tests, through a branch and a pull request as usual. Merging
    deploys it to Render, but it does nothing until switched on.

  2. You:
  - set LOG_CLIENT_IP_DIAGNOSTICS = true in Render;
  - run step a once more;
  - copy the 6 diagnostic lines from Render's Logs tab to me. They contain only
    counts and fingerprints, so they're safe to paste.

  3. Me: the fingerprint that stays the same across all 6 attempts shows exactly
  where your real address is. I then give you the right setting. If the address
  is only in a Cloudflare header, that needs a small code change instead, which
  I'd explain before making.

  4. Then:
  - switch diagnostics off;
  - rerun steps a, b and c to confirm;
  - record the result in the go-live record.

  Until it's fixed: the admin login has no rate limit. The other protections
  still hold: bcrypt slows each password check, a strong 12+ character password
  is required, and there's only one admin account. So this is a gap to close,
  not an emergency. TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS = 2 is no worse than 1, so you can leave
  it for now.

  merge until you approve, since merging deploys the backend.

    allow marker.

  log. They came from uvicorn's own access log, because my test server lacked

    changes when the service redeploys or restarts. The log you pasted shows

⏺ Those 6 lines show exactly where your address is. Here's what they say:

  Across all 6 attempts
  X-Forwarded-For entry 1 (leftmost): 61534b every time
  entry 2: changes: 6ae110, 87acff, fc7ce1, 1ab981
  entry 3 (rightmost): mostly 90f8f9, once 1dbec0
  CF-Connecting-IP: 61534b
  True-Client-IP: 61534b

  What this means:
  - Your real address is 61534b. It's the same in all 6 lines, and Cloudflare's
    own CF-Connecting-IP and True-Client-IP headers agree with it.
  - There are three hops, not one or two. The chain is your laptop → a 
    Cloudflare server → Render's proxy, so your address sits third from the 
    right.
  - Why 2 failed: with TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS = 2 the app read entry 2, which is a
    Cloudflare server and changes on almost every request. So each attempt
    looked like a new visitor, and the limit never triggered. With 1 it read
    Render's proxy, which also changes now and then.
  - Faking still won't help with 3. A faked X-Forwarded-For would only add
    entries to the left of your real address. Counting 3 from the right still
    lands on your real one. Step c will prove it.

  So the fix is one setting, no code change: TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS = 3.

  Next step: set 3, keeping diagnostics on for one more round

  Keep diagnostics on for now, so the confirmation run shows used(hops=3)
  matching your stable fingerprint.

  1. In Render, open Environment and change TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS from 2 to 3.
  2. Click Save, rebuild and deploy, and wait for "Your service is live".
  3. Reply "deployed", and I'll give you check 5's steps a, b and c again.

  After check 5 passes, we'll set LOG_CLIENT_IP_DIAGNOSTICS back to false (one
  more redeploy) and record everything in the go-live record.

  The cause, measured rather than guessed: visitors reach the app through three
  hops (Cloudflare, then Render's edge, then Render's internal proxy). So the
  correct setting is TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS = 3. With 1 or 2, the app read a
  Cloudflare or Render server address that changed on almost every request, and
  the login limit never triggered. The admin login is now properly rate-limited 
  on the live site.

  One small caveat about step b. It's strongest when done within the minute
  after step a, while the laptop is still blocked. If your phone login came
  later, b alone proves less. The diagnostic lines already show that each
  visitor is now keyed by their own Cloudflare-confirmed address, so I'm
  confident. If you'd like it airtight, repeat a then b within one minute some
  time.

  Go-live check 9, step 1 of 5: get ready (in your own Terminal app)

  Run these steps in the Terminal app (Applications → Utilities → Terminal), not
  here with !. Your database address must never come into this chat.

  Use one Terminal window for all five steps: the address you paste now is
  remembered only in that window.

  1. Make sure Docker Desktop is running, with the whale icon in the menu bar.
  2. Open Terminal and run these three lines:
  cd ~/projects/drone-tender-desk
  mkdir -p ~/drone-tender-desk-backups
  read -s "LIVE_DATABASE_URL?Paste the Neon DATABASE_URL, then press Enter: "; 
  echo
  3. At the prompt, paste the Neon connection string from your password manager
     and press Enter. Nothing appears as you paste; that's the hidden prompt
     working.
  4. To check it was stored without showing it, run:
  echo "${#LIVE_DATABASE_URL} characters stored"
     You should see a number of characters, typically about 100–150. It must not
     be 0.

  Reply with "step 1 done" and the character count. The count is safe to share;
  it reveals nothing about the address.

✻ Crunched for 2m 11s · done 7:17 PM

❯ step 1 done, 128 characters stored                                            
⏺ 128 characters is right for a Neon address, so it's stored.

  Step 2 of 5: back up the live database (same Terminal window)

  Paste this into the same window, all of it at once:

  PGURL="$LIVE_DATABASE_URL" docker run --rm -e PGURL -v 
  ~/drone-tender-desk-backups:/backup postgres:18 \
    sh -c 'pg_dump --format=custom --no-owner --no-acl 
  --file=/backup/tender_desk-$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M).dump "$PGURL"'
  ls -l ~/drone-tender-desk-backups

  What happens:
  - The first time, Docker downloads the postgres:18 image, which takes about a
    minute.
  - pg_dump then connects to Neon and writes the backup. If Neon's database was
    asleep, it wakes it first, which adds a few seconds.
  - The last line lists the folder. You should see a file like
    tender_desk-20261014-1352.dump. The size depends on your tender's PDF,
    probably a few hundred KB to a few MB.

  Keep that .dump file private. It's your backup, and it contains the admin's
  password hash.

  Reply with:
  - the file name and size from the ls -l line. These aren't secret.
  - any error lines, if it fails. First check they don't contain your address,
    though pg_dump errors normally show only the host name.

✻ Worked for 4s · done 7:17 PM

❯ step 2 done                                                                   

⏺ Good. You didn't mention the file size, so please include it in your next
  reply. A size of 0, or only a few KB, would mean the PDF didn't come through,
  so it's a useful sanity check.

  Step 3 of 5: prove a restore into a throwaway container (same Terminal window)

  This starts a temporary postgres:18 database that exists only inside Docker,
  with no published port, so nothing outside your Mac can reach it. It's deleted
  in step 5. It is not the live database and not your development database.

  Paste this into the same window:

  docker run -d --rm --name tender-desk-restore -e 
  POSTGRES_HOST_AUTH_METHOD=trust postgres:18
  until docker logs tender-desk-restore 2>&1 | grep -q "PostgreSQL init process 
  complete"; do sleep 1; done
  until docker exec tender-desk-restore pg_isready -U postgres -q; do sleep 1; 
  done
  DUMP=$(ls -t ~/drone-tender-desk-backups/*.dump | head -1)
  docker exec -i tender-desk-restore pg_restore -U postgres -d postgres 
  --no-owner --no-acl < "$DUMP" && echo "Restore finished"

  What happens:
  1. The first line prints a long container ID.
  2. The two until lines wait while the temporary database starts, about 5–10
     seconds.
  3. The restore runs, and the last line prints Restore finished.

  Reply with:
  - the file name and size from step 2;
  - whether you saw Restore finished;
  - any error lines, if they appear. Restore errors mention table or object
    names, never your address. If you see "container name already in use", tell
    me that instead.

     were wrong statements, corrected where they appear:

       qualifies a bidder, and eligibility is out of scope in v1.

       text, so for now only the category can be compared and everything else is
       "unknown".

       but still a stub, and that it extracts text, not numbers.

     Four were v1 decisions the proposal must fit or deliberately change.
     They're recorded in a new §11, "Relation to the current specs (v1)":
     - Accounts: one admin and no OEM login, so the smallest slice uses your
       admin as the evaluator, and OEM accounts come later;
     - Eligibility: "verifying eligibility" is out of scope, so badges mean
       reviewed evidence, not certification;
     - Hosting: Vercel Hobby is non-commercial only, and the database holds
       about 1 GB, so evidence stays as links;
     - Colours: amber already means "Closing soon".

       tightened to private on download, so I set the usual readable ones.
